[AGENT]: - The stack watch now tracks the thread’s current saved context before every arm, rather than keeping the address it captured back when the thread was created. I added `thread_stack_watch_default_suspect()` to derive the right address (prefer the latest `thread->context`, falling back to the base of the kernel stack) and use it whenever we activate or re-arm a watch (`src/kernel/process.c:561-607`, `src/kernel/process.c:863-892`, `src/kernel/process.c:897-903`). This means that after each context switch the guard protects the exact slot (RIP/RSP, etc.) that `switch_to_thread` will restore next time, so any write that zeroes it will trip the watch and log the culprit instead of slipping through. - Combined with the earlier fix (re-arming the watch for the thread that just yielded), we should now get a `[stack-write…]` or stack watch fault as soon as the timer/anyone scribbles on ping’s saved context rather than another resume anomaly with no clue. Build: - `make` Please rerun the ping stress. When the corruption happens, you should now see either a stack-watch delta or an explicit stack-write log identifying which routine touched that memory. That will give us the concrete culprit to fix next.

[AGENT]: Doesn’t look like a simple ordering bug in `switch_to_thread`. The sequence is: 1. Save/park the previous thread (FPU, FS/GS, etc.). 2. Mark the next thread runnable, drop stack guards, verify context hash, assert stack, etc. 3. Switch CR3, stack, FS/GS. 4. Call `context_switch(prev_ctx, next_ctx)` – this updates `prev->context` with the current RSP and loads `next->context`. 5. Once `context_switch` returns, we *already resumed* the previous thread that yielded; we’re now back in that thread (still named `prev`). Only after that return do we run the re-arm bookkeeping (thread stack watch, context guard update, deferred frees). So there’s no window where we “resume” a thread without restoring it first—the assembly `context_switch` does the full register swap. The fact we still see RIP zero means the saved stack frame for `ping` got clobbered *before* we tried to restore it, not that we skipped the restore. The repeated `[sched] stack watch armed thread=shell ... context=timer_tick` lines you captured show the stack guard is catching something writing into the shell thread’s context area each timer tick (address `0x21B1D00`). That’s separate from the ping thread’s saved context (`0x21D3E30`). It suggests some periodic code is still poking into other threads’ stacks despite the guard (probably logging or […]

This tells us the corruption happened *before* we try to resume the thread, so we’ll finally see which context-guard update (e.g., `switch_from` vs. others) encounters wiped state and can trace back to the guilty code path (`src/kernel/process.c:1700-1720`). When the corruption reappears you should see the new log message before the resume anomaly; that will give us the first concrete clue about *when* the stack got zeroed, which code path was updating the guard at that moment, and we can trace from there.

[AGENT]: Yeah, forcing every stack watch to default to the thread’s context pointer was too aggressive—turns out we ended up arming write-protect pages right where active kernel code (timer tick/interrupt prologue) legitimately writes the current thread’s save area, so we immediately tripped the guard in the shell before doing anything useful. I’ve undone that suspect defaulting: the stack watch now only tracks the address a caller explicitly requests (same as before), so we’re back to the prior stable behaviour. The context-guard logging we added is still there, so when the ping corruption reappears we’ll at least get a warning that RIP went zero during `context_guard_update` and know which label was running. Build: - `make` Please reboot/test again. If you see the ping anomaly, look for the new `[sched] context_guard update saw zero rip thread=… label=…]` line; that should tell us whether it’s happening during `switch_from`, the stack watch resume, etc., and we can chase the real culprit from there.

[AGENT]: - `switch_to_thread` now re-arms the stack-watch only for the thread we just descheduled (`prev`), restoring the original logic. After `context_switch` returns we’re running inside the resumed “next” thread, so guarding that thread’s stack immediately led to false alerts on boot; the fix goes back to arming the stack guard when a thread goes to sleep (`src/kernel/process.c:3648`). Build: - `make` With the scheduler stable again, please rerun the ping test and look for the new `[sched] context_guard update saw zero rip …]` warning when the corruption happens; that’ll tell us which code path is seeing the zeroed context so we can fix the real bug next.

[AGENT]: - Added a new kernel-side shell command `letters` (`src/sbin/cmd_letters.c`, declared in `include/shell_commands.h` and wired into `g_commands` in `src/sbin/shell.c:768`). It continuously prints the alphabet (one letter per line) with a 100 ms delay between letters, so you now have a deterministic long-running workload for stress tests or CTRL‑C experiments. - Reworked the shell’s input handling (`src/sbin/shell.c`): * Introduced a small typeahead ring buffer so characters typed while another command is running aren’t lost. * Added a non-blocking wait-hook (`shell_wait_hook_func`) that the shell registers before launching a foreground process; it drains raw keyboard/serial input, queues normal characters, and watches for CTRL‑C to kill the running process via `shell_request_interrupt`. * Updated `cli_read_line` to recognize CTRL‑C while you’re typing, print “^C” on both console/serial, clear the current line, and immediately reprompt. * Refactored the low-level character-reading helpers so both the main CLI loop and the wait-hook cooperate cleanly. - Build: `make` To try it out, boot, run `letters`, and hit CTRL‑C—expect the loop to terminate promptly and the prompt to return, even while other keys are typed ahead.

[AGENT]: - Added the new `letters` command (`src/sbin/cmd_letters.c`, declaration in `include/shell_commands.h`, registered in `src/sbin/shell.c`) which prints A–Z with a 100 ms pause and loops forever—ideal for stressing the scheduler/interrupt handling. - Implemented CTRL‑C handling without the intrusive typeahead buffer that caused the earlier crashes: * While typing at the prompt, hitting CTRL‑C now emits `^C`, clears the current line, and re‑prompts (`src/sbin/shell.c:1288`). * When the shell is waiting for a foreground command to finish, the new `shell_wait_hook_func` polls for CTRL‑C and kills the running process if it sees one; other keys typed during that period are ignored rather than buffered, keeping the code simple and stable (`src/sbin/shell.c:1249-1283`). * Supporting helper `shell_read_raw_char` consolidates the non‑blocking keyboard/serial reads, and `shell_emit_ctrl_c` centralizes the console/serial echo. Build: - `make` Please reboot the kernel, run `letters`, and press CTRL‑C to confirm it terminates cleanly; also verify typing CTRL‑C at the prompt behaves as expected.
